to the
\PZ(\Pell\Pell+\PGn\PGn)\PH(\PQb\PQb)

[
115
]
(
\PZ(\Pell\Pell)\PH(\PGt\PGt)

[
114
]
) analysis. The red and blue solid lines indicate the
product
\sigma\mathcal{B}
as expected by the 2HDM Type I and Type II models, respectively, for
the parameters
\tan\beta=3
and
\cos(\beta-\alpha)=0.1
. The shaded areas associated
with these predictions indicate the corresponding model uncertainties. The
results and model predictions have been adapted from Refs.
[
115
,
114
]
.

Figure
24
shows the upper limits on
\sigma(\Pp\Pp\to{\HepParticle{A}{}{}})\mathcal{B}({\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PZ\PH)

as functions of the mass of the
CP
-odd Higgs boson
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}
, using
\PH
decays to
\PQb\PQb

[
115
]

and
\PGt\PGt

[
114
]
, obtained with the data set recorded in 2016.
This plot also shows the expected cross sections for
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}
bosons in two typical 2HDM scenarios.
These feature a drop beyond the
\ttbar
threshold because of the
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\ttbar
channel opening up.

Figure 25:

Search for
\PX\to\PW\PH

for the production of a
\PWpr
spin-1

\mathcal{B}

Search for
\PX\to\PZ\PH

\mathcal{B}

decay.

Figures
25
and
26
show the upper limits
on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
for spin-1
\PWpr
and
\PZpr
resonances,
as a function of the masses
m_{\PWpr}
and
m_{\PZpr}
, respectively.
The limits are derived for DY (left) and VBF (right) production separately.
The exclusion limits reach values of
\sigma\mathcal{B}
below 0.1 and 0.3
\unit
fb
for the DY and VBF topologies, respectively.
In DY production the results from searches with leptons in the final state yield a stronger exclusion
for
m_{\PWpr}
masses below 1.7
\TeV
and
m_{\PZpr}
below 3.2
\TeV
.
For higher masses, the fully hadronic final state shows higher sensitivity.
The interpretations of these upper limits on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
in HVT models will be
discussed in detail in Section
0.4.3
.

0.3.2
The
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
decays

The six searches for
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
discussed in Section
0.2.4

target a variety of final states with
\PQb
jets, photons, light leptons, and
\tauh
leptons.
The searches study spin-0 and spin-2 resonances in the mass range 0.28–4.5
\TeV
. We denote the spin-0
resonance as
\PX
since interpretations in warped extra dimension and extended Higgs sector
models are both possible. We denote the spin-2 resonance as
\HepParticle
G
\Xspace
having a graviton in mind.

Figure 27:

Search for
\PX\to\PH\PH
/
{\HepParticle{G}{}{}}\Xspace\to\PH\PH
: Observed and expected 95%
\CL
upper
limits on the product of the cross section
\sigma
for the production of a
(left) spin-0 resonance
\PX
and (right) a spin-2 resonance
\HepParticle
G
\Xspace
, via
gluon-gluon fusion and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}
for the corresponding

{\PH\PH}
decay. The results of the individual analyses presented in this report,
corrected for the branching fractions of the respective H decay modes,
and the result of their combined likelihood analysis are shown. The observed
limits are indicated by markers connected with solid lines and
the expected limits by dashed lines.

Figure 28:

Search for
\PX\to\PH\PH
/
{\HepParticle{G}{}{}}\Xspace\to\PH\PH
: Observed and expected 95%
\CL
upper
limits on the product of the cross section
\sigma
for the production of a
(left) spin-0 resonance
\PX
and (right) a spin-2 resonance
\HepParticle
G
\Xspace
, via
gluon-gluon fusion, and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}
for the corresponding

{\PH\PH}
decay, as obtained from the combined likelihood analysis of all
contributing individual analyses presented in this report and shown in
Fig.
27
. In addition to the limit from the combined
likelihood analysis the 68 and 95% central intervals for the
expected upper limits in the absence of a signal are shown as colored bands.

Figure
27
shows the upper limits on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
as functions of the
resonance mass for both spin hypotheses.
The exclusion in terms of
\sigma\mathcal{B}

extends down to 0.2
\unit
fb for both spin scenarios probed. The best sensitivity at low masses
is obtained by the diphoton search, while at high masses the two searches with
\PQb
-tagged merged jets
show the best sensitivity.
The results of the statistical combination as described in Section
0.2.6
are
shown as red lines. These combined results are presented again separately in Fig.
28

along with the
\pm 1
and
\pm 2
s.d. intervals on the expected limits.
No deviation larger than 2 s.d. from the expected limits is observed.
Large improvements in sensitivity relative to the best individual channel are achieved
in the range of
m_{\PX}\sim 0.5
–1
\TeV
, where many channels contribute with about the same weight
to the combination. Below masses of 0.32
\TeV
and above 0.8
\TeV
, this combination gives the
strongest observed limits to date on resonant
{\PH\PH}
production. A recent combination
of
{\PH\PH}
searches performed by the ATLAS Collaboration can be found in Ref.
[
175
]
.

0.3.3
The
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH
decays

Three searches target the
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH
decay.
Two are dedicated to lower masses with the
\PH
boson decaying to
\PGg\PGg
or
\PGt\PGt
with two
\PQb
-tagged AK4 jets for the reconstruction of the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
boson (as discussed in Section
0.2.5
).
The search in the fully hadronic final state with two double-
\PQb
-tagged
AK8 jets targets the high-mass regime.
As the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
boson decays to
\PQb\PQb
in all cases considered, this allows for
a direct comparison of the results from these three searches,
without the assumption of a specific model.
Furthermore, this makes a model-independent combination possible, where only the
branching fractions of the
\PH
boson need to be taken into account.

Figure 29:

Search for
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH
: Observed and expected upper limits, at 95%
\CL
, on the
product of the cross section
\sigma
for the production of a resonance
\PX
via gluon-gluon fusion and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}
for the
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH
decay. For the branching fractions of the
\PH\to\PGt\PGt
,
\PH\to\gamma\gamma

and
\PH\to\PQb\PQb
decays, the SM values are assumed.
The results derived from the individual analyses presented in this
report and the result of their combined likelihood analysis are shown as
functions of
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
and
m_{\PX}
for
m_{\PX}\leq 1\TeV
. Observed limits are indicated by
markers connected with solid lines, expected limits by dashed lines. For presentation purposes, the limits
have been scaled in successive steps by two orders of magnitude, each. For
each set of graphs, a black arrow points to the
m_{\PX}
related legend.

Figure 30:

Search for
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH
: Observed and expected upper limits, at 95%
\CL
, on the
product of the cross section
\sigma
for the production of a resonance
\PX
via gluon-gluon fusion and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}
for the
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH
decay. For the branching fractions of the
\PH\to\PGt\PGt

and
\PH\to\PQb\PQb
decays, the SM values are assumed.
The results derived from the individual analyses presented in this
report and the result of their combined likelihood analysis are shown as
functions of
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
and
m_{\PX}
for
m_{\PX}\geq 1.2\TeV
. Observed limits are indicated by
markers connected with solid lines, expected limits by dashed lines. For presentation purposes, the
limits have been scaled in successive steps by four orders of magnitude, each.
For each set of graphs, a black arrow points to the
m_{\PX}
related legend.

Figures
29
and
30
show
the upper limits on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
as functions of the
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
for
m_{\PX}\leq 1\TeV
and
for
m_{\PX}\geq 1.2\TeV
, respectively. The results have been achieved
by adjusting each channel to the corresponding SM branching fraction
of the
\PH
boson decay under consideration. No correction has been made for the
unknown branching fraction of
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\to\PQb\PQb
, which is the same in all searches.

At low
m_{\PX}
, the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PGt\PGt)
and
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PGg\PGg)

analyses provide the best sensitivity. For
m_{\PX}=1\TeV
and higher,
the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PQb\PQb)
in the merged jet topology dominates for small and medium values of
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
.
At the largest values of
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
, however, approaching the kinematic limit,
the sensitivity of the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PQb\PQb)
analysis is reduced because the Lorentz boost of
the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
boson rest frame is too small for the fragmentation products of the two
\PQb
quarks
to merge into a single jet.

The three analyses are statistically combined as described in Section
0.2.6
,
and the resulting expected and observed limits are shown in Figs.
29
and

30
.
Covering the full mass grid, however, is beyond the scope of this Report.
This combination is shown as an example for the given mass points, and a separate publication
in the near future will include a full combination featuring a larger set of decay modes.
The typical exclusion upper limits on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
are about 50, 5, and 0.3
\unit
fb for

m_{\PX}=0.5
, 1, and 3
\TeV
, respectively.
No excess larger than two s.d. above the expected limit is observed at any of these mass points.
A two-dimensional representation of the experimental limits in the (
m_{\PX}
,
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
) parameter space is shown
as part of the interpretation in Section
0.4.1
.

0.4
Model-specific interpretation

We interpret the results of the individual searches and their combinations
in specific models. The interpretations highlight the coverage of the
analyses in the corresponding parameter space and show which regions
are excluded by the current data.
The first three subsections address how the measurements can constrain
the parameter space of models with an extended Higgs sector,
warped extra dimensions, and in a HVT framework.
Section
0.4.4

is dedicated to studies going beyond the narrow-width approximation (NWA),
where we investigate the effects of non-negligible resonance widths and
interference.

0.4.1
Extended Higgs sector models

The MSSM

The decays
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
and
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PZ\PH
can have sizable branching fractions
in models with two complex Higgs doublets. However, as discussed previously, the branching
fractions get suppressed when approaching the alignment limit, where the
\PH
boson becomes SM-like.
Searches for
{\PH\PH}
and
\PZ\PH
can nonetheless set important constraints in these models,
in particular at low to intermediate values of
\tanb
, and for masses below or near
the
\ttbar
threshold,
m_{\PX/{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}\lesssim 350
\GeV
.

Figure 31:

Interpretation of the results from the searches for the
\PX\to{\PH\PH}

\CL
, in the (
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}
,

{\PH\PH}

[

,

[

and

[

]
decays is shown.
Also shown, are the results from one representative search for
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PZ\PH

[

]
and indirect constraints obtained from measurements of
the coupling strength of the observed
\PH

[

]
. Results
not marked by a club symbol are based on an integrated luminosity of
35.9\fbinv
.

31
shows exclusion regions in the (
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}
,
\tanb
) plane of the hMSSM
[

,

,

,

]
.
For this and the following model interpretations, the version numbers of the corresponding tools are documented in
Ref.
[

]
.
The branching fractions are obtained with
HDECAY

[
178
,
179
]
.
The gluon fusion cross section is obtained with
SusHi

[

,

]
,
which includes higher-order QCD corrections
[

,

,

,

,

,

,

]
and EW effects from light quarks
[
189
,

]
.
The
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
searches result in an exclusion for
\tanb\lesssim 6
for
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}
just above the

{\PH\PH}
production threshold of 250
\GeV
, decreasing to
\tanb\lesssim 1
for
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}\approx 600\GeV
.
This is complementary to the exclusion regions from searches for fermionic decays, such as
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PGt\PGt
,
which exclude regions of large
\tanb
.
The
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PZ\PH
search in the
\PH\to\PGt\PGt
channel provides sensitivity for
220<m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}<350\GeV
and
excludes regions below
\tanb=3.6
for
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}\lesssim 330\GeV
.
Compared to other direct searches, there is a unique sensitivity of the
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
searches for

m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}\gtrsim 450\GeV
and
\tanb<5
.
At the same time, the constraints derived from the measurements of the
\PH
boson couplings are
somewhat more stringent, albeit these place only indirect constraints on this model.

Figure 32:

Interpretation of the results from the searches for the
\PX\to{\PH\PH}

[

[

[

]

{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PZ\PH

[

. The parameter

with 125
\GeV
within a 3
\GeV
margin is indicated by the dark hatched area.
Results not marked by a club symbol are based on an integrated luminosity
of
35.9\fbinv
.

The frequently used
M^{125}_{\text{h}}
benchmark model is not very suitable for interpretations of results from
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
searches
as these exclude regions at low
\tanb
where the SM-like scalar has a mass inconsistent with 125
\GeV
and thus with the observed
\PH
boson. Instead, we choose to interpret these results in the
M^{125}_{\text{h,EFT}}
scenario
[
67
,
69
]
.
Higgs boson masses and mixings are obtained with
FeynHiggs

[
191
,
192
,
15
,
193
,
194
,
195
,
196
,
197
]
. The branching fraction calculations
make use of both
FeynHiggs
,
HDECAY
, and
PROPHECY4f

[
198
,
199
]
.
The cross section for gluon fusion production is obtained from the same tools and predictions as in the hMSSM scenario.
The resulting exclusion regions in the (
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}
,
\tanb
) plane are shown in Fig.
32
.
In this scenario, the parameter regions excluded by the
{\PH\PH}
combination are found not to be in conflict with the measured

\PH
boson mass. For
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}\gtrsim 400\GeV
the results from the combination provide unique exclusions.
Otherwise, the overall picture is similar to the hMSSM scenario.

The 2HDM

Figure 33:

{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\to\PZ\PH(\PQb\PQb)

analysis
[

]
, in the (upper left) Type I, (upper

\CL
, in the plane defined by
\cos(\beta-\alpha)
and
\tanb
, are shown. The excluded
regions are represented by the shaded gray areas. The 68 and 95% central
intervals of the expected exclusion contours in the absence of a signal
are indicated by the green and yellow bands. Contours are derived from the
projection on the corresponding 2HDM parameter space for
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}=300\GeV
.
The regions of parameter space where the natural width of the
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}
boson

\Gamma_{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}
is comparable to or larger than the experimental resolution
and thus the narrow-width approximation is not valid are represented by
hatched gray areas. Figure from Ref.
[
115
]
.

Exclusion limits in the 2HDM are derived from the results of the search for
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}\Xspace\to\PZ\PH(\Pb\Pb)

[
115
]
.
The 2HDM cross sections and branching fractions are computed with
2HDMC

[
62
]

and
SusHi
, respectively. The light
\PH
boson mass is set to 125
\GeV
and
m_{\PX}=m_{\Hpm}=m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}
is used.
The
\PZ
boson branching fractions are set to the measured values
[
48
]
.
Figure
33
shows the constraints in the (
\tanb
,
\cos(\beta-\alpha)
) plane
for
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}=300\GeV

[
115
]
.
The search excludes nearly the whole region of low
\tanb
in all four 2HDM scenarios, except for a narrow region around
\cos(\beta-\alpha)=0

for which the branching fraction goes to zero, another narrow region at negative
\cos(\beta-\alpha)
for the Type I and lepton-specific scenarios, and at positive
\cos(\beta-\alpha)
for the other two scenarios.
At high
\tanb
, the excluded region widens in the Type II and flipped scenarios,
whereas there is no sensitivity in the Type I and lepton-specific scenarios because the production
cross section for the
{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}
boson becomes too small.

The NMSSM and TRSM models

The searches for
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH
decays are interpreted in the NMSSM
and TRSM models. In both models, there are various free parameters
besides the masses of the additional
\PX
and
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
bosons that affect
the cross sections and branching fractions. To check whether the
searches are sensitive to a point in the (
m_{\PX}
,
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
) plane, a parameter
scan is performed to determine the maximally allowed cross section,
taking all previous constraints on the models into account.

In the NMSSM case, we obtain the maximally allowed cross section values
for
\sigma(\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH\to\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb)
from the scans in
Ref.
[
200
]
, which are based on version 5.6.2 of the
program
NMSSMTools

[
200
,
201
]
.
These numbers are divided by the corresponding branching fraction

\mathcal{B}(\PH\to\PQb\PQb)
to obtain an approximation for the maximally allowed
values of
\sigma(\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH)
. Uncertainties arising from the
precision of the measured branching fractions of the
\PH
boson are
neglected.

Figure 34:

(Upper left) Observed and (upper right) expected upper limits at
95%
\CL
, on the product of the cross section
\sigma
for the production of
a resonance
\PX
via gluon-gluon fusion and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}
for
the
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH
decay, as obtained from a combined likelihood analysis
of the individual analyses presented in this report and shown in
Fig.
29
. The results are presented in a plane
defined by
m_{\PX}
and
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
. The limits have
been evaluated in discrete steps corresponding to the centers of the boxes.
The numbers in the boxes are given in
\unit
fb.
The corresponding maximally allowed values of
\sigma\mathcal{B}
in the NMSSM are
also shown for comparison (lower plot), as adapted from
Ref.
[
200
]
.

Figure
34
shows the observed and expected
upper limits at 95%
\CL
on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
of the combined
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH

searches (upper panel), together with the maximally allowed model
values (lower panel). While the experimental limits appear to touch
the model predictions in several places, there is not much additional
exclusion. This is expected because many relevant measurements,
including the CMS searches for
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PGt\PGt)
and

\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PQb\PQb)
presented in this article, are already
accounted for in this version of
NMSSMTools
, which lowers the
maximally allowed NMSSM cross sections correspondingly. Therefore, no
new constraints are expected from these channels compared to those in
the original publications
[
167
,
123
,
124
]
.

Comparisons of the measured limits for
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PQb\PQb)
in
merged final states with the maximally allowed TRSM values can be
found in Ref.
[
124
]
. The measurement excludes part of the
allowed TRSM parameter space in a wedge-shaped region between
m_{\PX}\approx 1000
–1300
\GeV
and around
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}\approx 125\GeV
. An
interpretation of the
\PX\to{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PGt\PGt)
measurements
within the TRSM benchmark planes can be found in
Ref.
[
202
]
.

The real-singlet extension

The additional scalar boson
\PX
predicted in the real-singlet model has the same relative
couplings to SM particles as the SM
\PH
boson.
Most searches for
\PX\to\PH\PH
assume that the width of the
\PX
boson is much smaller
than the reconstructed mass resolution, such that the NWA holds.
We use the real-singlet model for a dedicated study of nonnegligible width and interference effects
and present the results in Section
0.4.4
.
The corresponding model interpretations for the
\PX\to\PH\PH
combination in the real-singlet model are presented there.

0.4.2
Warped extra dimensions

Figure 35:

Observed and expected limits, at 95%
\CL
, on the parameters of

\HepParticle

m_{{\HepParticle{G}{}{}}\Xspace}
. Excluded areas are indicated by the direction of the hatching along
the exclusion contours.

. The exclusion contours obtained

[

,

[
177
]
,

\PW(\Pell\PGn)\PW(\PQq\PQq)

[
116
]
,

\PV(\PQq\PQq)\PV(\PQq\PQq)

[
118
]
, and

\PZ(\PGn\PGn)\PZ(\PQq\PQq)

[

]
, in case of the radion
interpretation, and from individual searches in the decays

\PZ(\PQq\PQq)\PZ(\Pell\Pell)

,

, in the case of the graviton

The measured upper limits on resonant
{\PH\PH}
production can also be interpreted
in the context of WED models (as discussed in Section
0.1.2
).
Figure
35
(left) shows the lower limit on the bulk radion ultraviolet cutoff
parameter
\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}}
as a function of the radion mass
m_{{\HepParticle{R}{}{}}\Xspace}
for all
presented
{\PH\PH}
analyses and their combination.
The individual analyses with the best sensitivity are from the searches of

\PX\to\PH(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PGg\PGg)
for
m_{\PX}\lesssim 1\TeV
, and

\PX\to\PH(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PQb\PQb)
for
m_{\PX}\gtrsim 1\TeV
.
In the regions
0.5\lesssim m_{\PX}\lesssim 1\TeV
and
1\lesssim m_{\PX}\lesssim 1.5\TeV
,
the
\PX\to\PH(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PGt\PGt)
and
\PX\to\PH(\PQb\PQb)\PH(\PW\PW)
analyses contribute
significantly to the combination.
In the mass region below 1
\TeV
, the expected lower limit from the combination
ranges from 8 to 10
\TeV
, with observed limits reaching up to 12
\TeV
.
The strongest exclusion limits of about 12
\TeV
expected and 16
\TeV
observed are
reached near
m_{{\HepParticle{R}{}{}}\Xspace}=1.2\TeV
. The combination improves the sensitivity
over the full mass range probed.
Figure
35
(right) shows the corresponding
upper limits of the parameter
\tilde{k}
of the spin-2 bulk graviton
\HepParticle
G
\Xspace
.
The combination excludes values of
\tilde{k}
larger than about 0.3 at 95%
\CL
for the large mass range
0.3<m_{{\HepParticle{G}{}{}}\Xspace}<1.5\TeV
.

We compare the limits obtained from the
{\PH\PH}
combination with limits from searches
for
\PX\to\PZ\PZ

[
205
,
203
,
204
]
and

\PX\to\PW\PW

[
177
,
116
,
118
]
in Fig.
36
.
The
{\PH\PH}
combination is found to be very competitive, and it places
stronger constraints on the WED models in some mass regions.
For radions, shown on the left, the
{\PH\PH}
combination
shows about the same sensitivity as the
\PZ(\Pell\Pell)\PZ(\PQq\PQq/\PGn\PGn/\Pell\Pell)
final state
[
203
]

for
m_{{\HepParticle{R}{}{}}\Xspace}\lesssim 1\TeV
. The
{\PH\PH}
combination has the best sensitivity in the
region
1<m_{{\HepParticle{R}{}{}}\Xspace}<2\TeV
, and for higher masses it has a comparable sensitivity
as searches in final states from hadronic and semileptonic
\PW\PW
decays
[
116
,
118
]
.
For gravitons, the
{\PH\PH}
combination places the best upper limits on

\tilde{k}
for
250<m_{{\HepParticle{G}{}{}}\Xspace}<450\GeV
and
700<m_{{\HepParticle{G}{}{}}\Xspace}<2000\GeV
.

0.4.3
Heavy vector triplet models

The three searches for
\PX\to\PV\PH
introduced in Section
0.2.3
probe for
a new vector boson
\PV^{\prime}
(either
\PWpr
or
\PZpr
) in final states with 0, 1, and 2 leptons.
The resulting upper limits on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
presented in Section
0.3.1

are now interpreted in the HVT model.
The theoretical cross sections are calculated at NLO in QCD with the models
detailed in Ref.
[
28
,
30
]
.
The theory predictions with the corresponding
\mathcal{B}(\PWpr\to\PW\PH
) and
\mathcal{B}(\PZpr\to\PZ\PH
)
in the models A, B, and C, where couplings of
\PV^{\prime}
to bosons are enhanced,
have been shown in Figs.
25
and
26
.
The upper limits on
\sigma\mathcal{B}
are translated into lower limits on the vector boson masses.
The
\PWpr
and
\PZpr
masses are excluded up to 4.1 and 3.9
\TeV
,
respectively, in model B interpretations.

Figure
37
shows the upper limits on the DY production cross section of

\PV^{\prime}
in the
\PW\PH
and
\PZ\PH
channels, compared to those obtained from

\PV\PV

[
116
,
118
,
205
,
204
]
and fermion pair production
channels
[
206
,
207
,
208
,
209
]
assuming branching fractions
of the HVT models A and B. The corresponding theory predictions are overlaid.
The all-jets channels are sensitive to both
\PWpr
and
\PZpr
production and are thus interpreted in
combined
\PV^{\prime}
production. While in model A, searches for fermion pair production dominate the sensitivity,
in model B, where couplings of
\PV^{\prime}
to bosons are large, the
\PV\PV
and
\PV\PH
searches are most sensitive.

. The corresponding results are shown in Fig.

.

0.4.4
Effects of finite width and interference in resonant
{\PH\PH}
production

Most of the
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
analyses performed by the CMS Collaboration make use of the NWA,
where the width of BSM particles is neglected and no interference with nonresonant
Higgs boson pair production occurs.
However, in general, interference effects can strongly impact
the
{\PH\PH}
cross section
[
210
,
211
]
.
The interference can be either constructive or destructive, enhancing or decreasing the
{\PH\PH}
production rate
[
212
,
213
]
, and have a nonnegligible effect
in BSM exclusion limits.
We study the impact of the interference between nonresonant and resonant production in
the inclusive
\Pp\Pp\to{\PH\PH}
production, which can receive contributions from resonant
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
production.
This work provides the first measure of interference effects, identifying phase space regions
where the NWA is valid.
We use as a benchmark a simplified scenario based on the real-singlet model introduced in
Section
0.1.2
, as it includes the smallest number of additional free
parameters
[
214
]
.
We note that interference effects are model dependent and may be different for other BSM scenarios.

For this specific study, we modify the singlet model by not imposing the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.
The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry precludes terms of odd powers of the additional singlet scalar field, which are known
to be responsible for a stronger first-order EW phase transition
[
215
,
216
]
.
Exploiting EW symmetry breaking on the singlet model scalar potential, we are left with two mixing states.
After mass diagonalization, the identification of one of the states with the SM
\PH
boson
reduces the number of uncorrelated parameters further from seven to five.
The other state is associated with a new particle
\PX
.
The couplings of the
\PH
boson and the
\PX
particle are given by

g_{\PH kk}=g^{\text{SM}}_{\PH kk}\cos\alpha\quad\text{and}\quad g_{\PX kk}=-g^{\text{SM}}_{\PH kk}\sin\alpha,

(11)

where
\alpha
is the mixing angle, and
k
represents any SM particle.
For
m_{\PX}>2m_{\PH}
the width of the
\PX
resonance can be calculated as

\Gamma_{\PX}=\sin^{2}\alpha\,\Gamma^{\text{SM}}(m_{\PX})+\frac{\lambda_{{\PH\PH}\PX}^{2}\sqrt{1-4m_{\PH}^{2}/m_{\PX}^{2}}}{8\pi m_{\PX}},

(12)

where
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
is the trilinear coupling between two
\PH
bosons and the new particle
\PX
,
and
\Gamma^{\text{SM}}(m_{\PX})
represents the width of a scalar boson of mass
m_{\PX}
with the same
decay modes as the SM
\PH
boson.
The latter has been calculated by interpolating the values published in Ref.
[
35
]
.
In addition to
\alpha
,
m_{\PX}
and
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
, this singlet model also depends on the trilinear

\PH
coupling modifier
k_{\lambda}\equiv\lambda_{\PH\PH\PH}/\lambda^{\text{SM}}_{\PH\PH\PH}
, and on an additional scalar coupling.

We use the
\MGvATNLO
generator version 2.9.7
[
217
]
,
to simulate inclusive
{\PH\PH}
events in the singlet model at LO.
A custom universal
FeynRules

[
218
]
output (UFO) model based on Ref.
[
215
]

adds a heavy scalar boson to the SM with couplings to SM particles as defined in Eq. (
11
).
The samples are created according to the following parameter grid with
k_{\lambda}=1
:

•

m_{\PX}
[​
\GeV
]: 280, 300, 400, 500, 600, 700, 800, 900, 1000,

•

\sin\alpha
: 0.00, 0.10, 0.20, 0.30, 0.40, 0.50, 0.60, 0.70, 0.80, 0.90, 0.95, 0.99,

•

\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
[​
\GeV
]:
-
600,
-
500,
-
400,
-
300,
-
200,
-
100,
-
50, 0, 50, 100, 200, 300, 400, 500, 600,

where
m_{\PX}
is chosen based on the signal samples used in the
{\PH\PH}
combination presented in Section
0.3.2
.
The resonant, nonresonant, and total cross sections for each combination of grid points are generated separately.
We perform a parameter scan in the parameters
m_{\PX}
,
\sin\alpha
, and
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
of the interference ratio defined as

R_{\text{int}}=\frac{\sigma^{\text{full}}-\left(\sigma^{\text{resonant-only}}+\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}}\right)}{\sigma^{\text{resonant-only}}+\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}}}.

(13)

We obtain the nonresonant cross section by setting the coupling
g_{\PX kk}
defined in Eq. (
11
) to zero,
and the resonant-only cross section by setting the coupling
g_{\PH kk}
to zero.
The variable
R_{\text{int}}
provides information concerning the relative strength of the interference between the SM and
BSM processes. The larger the deviation of
R_{\text{int}}
from zero, the stronger the modification of the cross
section due to the interference. We consider the gluon fusion production mode due to its dominant contribution
to the cross section.
The UFO model and procedure are validated using the program
hpair

[
219
,
213
]

where the results varying
k_{\lambda}
in the nonresonant scenario are found to agree with the
NLO predictions of Ref.
[
4
]
.

Figure 41:

Contours of the variable
R_{\text{int}}
as defined in Eq. (
13
) and discussed
in the text, in the (
\sin\alpha
,
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
) plane for the singlet
model with
k_{\lambda}=1
and different resonance masses
m_{\PX}
between (upper
left) 280 and (lower right) 800
\GeV
. Contours are shown for
R_{\text{int}}
values
of (dashed blue)
-0.2
, (solid blue)
-0.1
, (solid green)
+0.1
, and (dashed green)

+0.2
. Regions that are excluded, at 95%
\CL
, from the combined likelihood
analysis of the
{\PH\PH}
analyses presented in this report are indicated by red
filled areas. Dashed black lines indicate constant relative widths of 5, 10,
and 20%.

Exact conclusions from this study naturally depend on the allowed size of
R_{\text{int}}
and the relative width
\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}
.
In the following, we choose as benchmark points
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 10
and
\pm 20\%
, and
\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}=5
, 10 and 20%.
The corresponding contours and exclusion limits derived from the
{\PH\PH}
combination in the singlet
model are shown in Fig.
41
.

Contours of positive (green) and negative (blue) interference ratios are shown
as solid
(for
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 10\%
) and dashed (for
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 20\%
) lines.
They are found to swap positions at
m_{\PX}=400\GeV
,
likely because of the peak of the nonresonant
{\PH\PH}
distribution.
The dotted lines denote coupling value combinations beyond which
the relative width of the resonance,
\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}
, exceeds 5
and 10%, respectively, implying the narrow width
approximation not being accurate anymore.
For a given
m_{\PX}
, the quadratic dependence of
\Gamma_{\PX}
on both
\sin\alpha
and
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
according to Eq. (
12
) leads to elliptical isolines of constant
\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}
.
The experimental bound from the
{\PH\PH}
combination discussed in Section
0.3.2

is obtained from the 95%
\CL
upper limit on
\sigma(\Pp\Pp\to\PX)\mathcal{B}(\PX\to{\PH\PH})
,
with the
\PX
production cross section growing with increasing
\sin\alpha
,
and
\mathcal{B}(\PX\to{\PH\PH})
growing with increasing
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
.
We note that large values of
\sin\alpha
, corresponding to regions where the
\PH
boson is less SM-like,
also tend to be excluded by precision measurements of the
\PH
boson
[
215
]
.

Figure 42:

Expected differential cross sections for
{\PH\PH}
production, as a function of

m_{{\PH\PH}}
, for the real-singlet model with
m_{\PX}=280\GeV
and

\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}=5\%
. The parameters
\sin\alpha
and
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
have been
chosen such that (upper row)
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 10\%
and (lower row)
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 20\%
,
for (left) negative and (right) positive values of
R_{\text{int}}
. The total
cross section for
{\PH\PH}
production
\sigma^{\text{full}}
(red line, labelled
as
\sigma_{\text{full}}
) is compared to the cross sections
\sigma^{\text{
resonant-only}}
(blue line, labelled as
\sigma_{\text{res}}
) and
\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}}
(green line, labelled as
\sigma_{\text{nonres}}
)
considering only resonant and nonresonant production. In the lower panels the
ratio of
\sigma^{\text{full}}
over
(\sigma^{\text{resonant-only}}+\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}})
is shown.

Figure 43:

Expected differential cross sections for
{\PH\PH}
production, as a function of

m_{{\PH\PH}}
for the real-singlet model with
m_{\PX}=500\GeV
and

\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}=5\%
. The parameters
\sin\alpha
and
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
have been
chosen such that (upper row)
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 10\%
and (lower row)
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 20\%
,
for (left) negative and (right) positive values of
R_{\text{int}}
. The total
cross section for
{\PH\PH}
production
\sigma^{\text{full}}
(red line, labelled
as
\sigma_{\text{full}}
) is compared to the cross sections
\sigma^{\text{
resonant-only}}
(blue line, labelled as
\sigma_{\text{res}}
) and
\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}}
(green line, labelled as
\sigma_{\text{nonres}}
)
considering only resonant and nonresonant production. In the lower panels the
ratio of
\sigma^{\text{full}}
over
(\sigma^{\text{resonant-only}}+\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}})
is shown.

For most of the studied mass points, sizable interference ratios occur
only in parameter regions to which the current measurements are either
not yet sensitive, or at too large values of
\sin\alpha
. In
particular, for large resonance masses, where interference effects tend
to grow, they are far below the current sensitivity and might only
play a role when the full data set from HL-LHC becomes
available
[
220
]
, as discussed in
Section
0.5.3
. However, there are
regions at intermediate
m_{\PX}
where the interpretation of NWA-based
limits for
{\PH\PH}
derived in the singlet model would solicit some care
already in the Run 2 combination (
\eg
,
m_{\PX}=500\GeV
,
\sin\alpha=0.2
and
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}=400\GeV
). It is important to note that
such interpretations are generally model dependent.

The differential cross sections as a function of
m_{{\PH\PH}}
are shown for representative points from
the (
\sin\alpha
,
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
) parameter space in
Fig.
42
for
m_{\PX}=280\GeV
, and in Fig.
43
for
m_{\PX}=500\GeV
.
The parameters are chosen such that
\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}=5\%
, which is well below the detector resolution for resonance masses below 1
\TeV
,
and
R_{\text{int}}=\pm 10\%
or
\pm
20%, so that sizable interference effects are expected.
The lineshapes show points in parameter space where the
R_{\text{int}}
contours intersect with lines of
constant
\Gamma_{\PX}/m_{\PX}=5\%
in Fig.
41
.

The mass points of
m_{\PX}=280
and 500
\GeV
have been chosen because these values are
on the left- and right-hand side of the peak in the
m_{{\PH\PH}}
distribution for nonresonant SM
{\PH\PH}
production.
The total signal contribution of the resonance, including the interference effect,
can be assessed as the difference between
\sigma^{\text{full}}
(red graph) and
\sigma^{\text{nonresonant}}
(green graph).
In the
m_{\PX}=280\GeV
case, the resonance peak is at a mass where the non-resonant background is low in comparison; hence the central part of the peak is not much affected in its shape, and a classical bump hunt should still work. However, the total cross section is modified as specified by
R_{\text{int}}
. For a precision measurement, which is not yet in our reach, a distortion of the signal shape, either a peak-dip or peak-tail pattern depending on the relative sign of the amplitudes, would have to be taken into account.
At
m_{\PX}=500\GeV
, in the top panels of Fig.
43
, the signal shape is found to be strongly modified by the interference effect. However, this occurs in a parameter region still relatively far away from the regions currently probed, as can be seen in Fig.
41
.
Although the expected interference effects clearly depend on the underlying model,
they can be expected to be of mounting importance in the future as the LHC data set increases.

0.5
Discovery potential at the HL-LHC

The HL-LHC
[
221
]
is planned to start in 2029 and aims to deliver a
\Pp\Pp
collision
data set corresponding to about 3000
\fbinv
of integrated luminosity in the baseline scenario, and up
to 4000
\fbinv
in the ultimate scenario, at an unprecedented center-of-mass energy of 14
\TeV
.
The CMS detector will be upgraded to cope with the large size of 140 (200) PU events on
average for the baseline (ultimate) scenario. The upgraded detector will also meet the
challenges from the adverse effects due to the radiation dose to which the detector components
are exposed, which is one order of magnitude higher than at the current LHC. Furthermore, major
improvements of the software for the online and offline event reconstruction are under development
to fully exploit the potential of the upgraded detector. Searches for scalars
\PX
decaying to
{\PH\PH}
or
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
are among the most relevant targets of research at the HL-LHC, and thus projection studies are very important
to motivate the ongoing hardware and software upgrades. Meanwhile, such studies can provide an
estimate of the sensitivity to the relevant BSM theories which can be achieved with the HL-LHC data.

This section describes the perspectives for the searches for
\PX
boson resonances decaying to
{\PH\PH}
or
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
at the HL-LHC,
in the most sensitive decay channels
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
,
\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt
, and
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
, in the baseline scenario of the HL-LHC.
Using the combined likelihood method, individual channels are statistically combined to exploit their
complementarity in sensitivity to different regions in parameter space of the tested BSM theories.
The expected upper limits at 95%
\CL
on the cross sections of the BSM processes of interest are
provided as functions of the masses of the BSM scalars. The expected exclusion in the parameters of
the relevant BSM theories is estimated, as well as the expected discovery significance for benchmark BSM signals.

0.5.1
Methodology for estimation of the discovery potential

The projection studies are based on the resonant
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
searches in the most
sensitive channels from the CMS Run 2 data set corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 138
\fbinv
,
as summarized in Table
0.5.1
. Descriptions of the Run 2
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
searches
are given in the sections indicated in the table.

\topcaption

Searches for resonant
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
production considered for the projection study.

Final state

Reference

Section

\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt

[
122
]

0.2.5

\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg

[
123
]

0.2.5

\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
(merged-jet)

[
124
]

0.2.5

Using the same approach as studies in Ref.
[
220
]
,
searches using the Run 2 data set are projected to an integrated
luminosity of 3000
\fbinv
. Where appropriate, the signal cross sections
have been scaled to the center-of-mass energy of
14
\TeV

[
35
]
.
As the upgraded CMS detector will ensure a
performance comparable to Run 2, the efficiency in the reconstruction and identification of photons,
leptons, jets and
\PQb
jets, as well as the resolution in their energy and momentum measurements are
assumed to be unchanged. The experimental sensitivity expected at the HL-LHC is derived using the
following three systematic uncertainty scenarios.

S1:

All the systematic uncertainties are assumed to remain the same as in Run 2. This is
an over-conservative scenario because the CMS detector upgrade, the progress in the reconstruction
techniques, and the very large data set available for the experimental calibrations are expected to
bring a substantial reduction of several systematic uncertainties. Furthermore, progress in the theory
calculations is expected to reduce the uncertainties in the predictions.

S2:

The theory uncertainties are halved, while the experimental uncertainties are set according
to the recommendations of Ref.
[
222
]
.

Statistical only:

The results are derived considering only the statistical uncertainty in data.

The projected results from the channels considered are statistically combined following the same
procedure as adopted for the Run 2 combination which is described in Section
0.2.6
.
In particular, the systematic uncertainties affecting multiple channels, such as the uncertainties
in the luminosity and on the
\PQb
jet identification efficiency, are treated as correlated among
all the input channels.

0.5.2
Discovery potential for
\PX\to{\PH\PH}

Figure 44:

Expected upper limits at 95%
\CL
, on the product of the cross section for
the production of a spin-0 resonance
\PX
and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}(\PX\to\PH\PH)
, as functions of
m_{\PX}
from the (upper left)
\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt

[
122
]
,
(upper right)
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg

[
123
]
, and (lower)
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
with two merged

\PQb\PQb
jets
[
124
]
analyses discussed in this report, projected to
an integrated luminosity of 3000
\fbinv
under the assumption of different
systematic uncertainty scenarios, as discussed in the text. All estimates
include the anticipated statistical uncertainties.

The expected upper limits at 95%
\CL
on the
\PX\to{\PH\PH}
cross section from the channels
considered projected to 3000
\fbinv
are shown for the three different systematic uncertainty
scenarios in Fig.
44
.
The projected upper limits from the
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
decay mode range between 60 and 3
\unit
fb for

m_{\PX}
within 300–1000
\GeV
.
The overall impact of the systematic uncertainties on the
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
upper limits is below 1%
because of the small uncertainty on the background modeling thanks to the estimation procedure
based on the fit to the data in sideband regions.

The
\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt
channel provides upper limits on the cross section at 95%
\CL
between 300 and 7
\unit
fb
for
m_{\PX}
within 300–1000
\GeV
. The systematic uncertainty with the largest impact in the S1 scenario
has its origin in the limited size of the MC simulation used for the background estimation.
In the S2 scenario, the statistical uncertainties on the simulated events are assumed to be negligible
and the main systematic uncertainties arise from the efficiencies of the
\PQb
jet and
\PGt
identification and misidentification.

The
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
channel in the boosted regime, in the following simply referred to as
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
, covers
m_{\PX}
values between 900 and 4000
\GeV
, and is
expected to provide upper limits between 0.04 and 0.02
\unit
fb at an integrated luminosity of 3000
\fbinv
.
The impact of the systematic uncertainties is very small, as the sensitivity of the analysis
is mainly limited by the statistical uncertainty in the data.

Figure 45:

Expected upper limits at 95%
\CL
, on the product of the cross section for
the production of a spin-0 resonance
\PX
and the branching fraction
\mathcal{B}(\PX\to\PH\PH)
, as a function of
m_{\PX}
, for an integrated luminosity of 3000
\fbinv
and the combination of the three analyses shown in
Fig.
44
. Shown are the
effects of the different systematic uncertainty scenarios (left), and
the reach of the individual analyses for the S2 systematic scenario (right).
All estimates include the anticipated statistical uncertainties.

The results of the combination of the resonant
{\PH\PH}
searches considered are shown in Fig.
45

in the different systematic uncertainty scenarios, and in comparison to the results from the individual channels.
The
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
channel is found to dominate the sensitivity in the region
m_{\PX}<500\GeV
; around 900
\GeV
the channel
with the best sensitivity is
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
, followed by
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
and
\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt
. For
m_{\PX}>1000\GeV
the only channel
considered is
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
which is expected to be the most sensitive in this kinematic region.
Thanks to the small impact of the systematic uncertainties on the
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
and
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
channels,
the differences between the three systematic uncertainty scenarios are rather small.

Perspectives for the discovery of BSM benchmark signals

The expected significance for the discovery of a benchmark BSM signal from a spin-0 resonance with a
mass of 1
\TeV
is calculated for several signal cross sections and represented as a function of the integrated
luminosity in Fig.
46
.

Figure 46:

Expected discovery significance for a spin-0 resonance
\PX
with
m_{\PX}=1\TeV

and cross sections of 1 and 10
\unit
fb, obtained for the combined likelihood
analysis of the resonant
{\PH\PH}
searches as discussed in Section
0.5

and shown in Figs.
44

and
45
, shown as function of the integrated
luminosity.

Based on the three channels considered in this projection, the significance of a signal of

\PX\to{\PH\PH}
with a cross section of 10
\unit
fb corresponds to about three standard deviations
at Run 2, while an integrated luminosity of 300
\fbinv
would yield 4.8
standard deviations, indicating an attractive discovery potential
already for Run 3 and its combination with Run 2. The significance of
the same signal would reach about 15 standard deviations at 3000
\fbinv
. A signal with a cross section
of 3
\unit
fb would be sufficient to reach an observation at the level of five standard deviations with 3000
\fbinv
.

Perspectives for MSSM scenarios

The projected exclusion limits at 95%
\CL
of the hMSSM and
M^{125}_{\text{h,EFT}}
benchmark scenarios from
resonant
{\PH\PH}
searches are shown in Fig.
47
.
The S1 systematic uncertainty scenario is used for the Run 2 result and conservatively also for the result with 300
\fbinv
,
while the S2 systematic uncertainty scenario is used for the projected 1000 and 3000
\fbinv
results.
Over the full accessible range in
\tanb
, the exclusion in
m_{{\HepParticle{A}{}{}}}
increases by about 250–300
\GeV
when moving from the Run 2 integrated luminosity to 3000
\fbinv
, for both the hMSSM and
M^{125}_{\text{h,EFT}}
scenarios.
This exclusion from the resonant
{\PH\PH}
searches will complement the searches for
\PX
decaying to a
pair of fermions or vector bosons.

.

.

Perspectives for the WED bulk scenario

The expected lower limits at 95%
\CL
on the bulk radion parameter
\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}}
as a function of
the radion mass
m_{{\HepParticle{R}{}{}}\Xspace}
are shown in Fig.
48
. The limits are obtained from the
combination of resonant
{\PH\PH}
searches in the WED bulk scenario.
The S1 systematic uncertainty scenario is used for the Run 2 result and conservatively also for the result with 300
\fbinv
,
while the S2 systematic uncertainty scenario is used for the projected 1000 and 3000
\fbinv
results.
Over the full range in
m_{{\HepParticle{R}{}{}}\Xspace}
, the limit on
\Lambda_{\mathrm{R}}
is expected to increase by a factor of at least two
with the full HL-LHC data set.

.

Perspectives for the singlet scenarios

In the singlet model of Section
0.4.4
with
k_{\lambda}=1
,
limits are derived in the (
\sin\alpha
,
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
) plane from the combination of resonant
{\PH\PH}
searches.
Resonance masses between 280 and 800
\GeV
are probed using Run 2 data and projected to
integrated luminosities corresponding to 300, 1000, and 3000
\fbinv
.
Projected exclusion regions at 95%
\CL
are shown in Fig.
49
.
The HL-LHC dataset of 3000
\fbinv
has the potential to considerably expand the present exclusion regions in
the (
\sin\alpha
,
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
) plane for all values of
m_{\PX}
.
Compared to the present limits, the largest improvement is observed for large masses,
m_{\PX}=600\GeV
and
higher, where large regions of the (
\sin\alpha
,
\lambda_{\PH\PH\PX}
) plane can be probed.

Figure 49:

Exclusion contours at 95%

, in the (

,

) plane
for

in the real-singlet model. These contours
are obtained from the combined likelihood analysis of the
{\PH\PH}
searches
discussed in Section
0.4.1
for (upper
left to lower right)
m_{\PX}=280
, 400, 500, 600, 700, and 800
\GeV
.
The expected limits from the Run 2 dataset have been projected to integrated
luminosities of 300, 1000, and 3000
\fbinv
. Excluded areas are indicated by
the direction of the hatching along the exclusion contours.

\PX\to
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}

\PX\to{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
are also projected to an integrated luminosity of

\fbinv
for the three systematic uncertainty scenarios.
The projections are derived for the individual channels in the
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
,
\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt
, and
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
final states,
and for a combination with the assumption of SM

\PX\to{\PH\PH}
projections.

The results of the
\PX\to{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
projections are presented in Figs.
50

and
51
for
m_{\PX}
up to and above 1
\TeV
, respectively.
The regions of the (
m_{\PX}
,
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
) parameter space with the largest ratios of
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}/m_{\PX}
correspond to a

{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
particle with low transverse momentum, and can be probed with the
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
channel.
In the regions with small ratios of
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}/m_{\PX}
, the
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
particle receives a large Lorentz boost,
such that the
\PQb\PQb\PQb\PQb
boosted channel has the highest sensitivity and only this final state is considered.
In the intermediate region, the
\PQb\PQb\PGg\PGg
and
\PQb\PQb\PGt\PGt
channels provide comparable sensitivity and about
equal weight in the combination.

Selected bins of the projections from the
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
combination are used for presenting expected upper limits as
functions of
m_{\PX}
and
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}
, and are shown in Fig.
52
.
In comparison with Fig.
34
, the improvement is clearly visible.

\CL
on the

\fbinv
. The NMSSM model scans are obtained with

version 5.6.2
[

]
, as described in Section

, and take

shows the projected exclusion contours for the final states

. This indicates a huge increase in sensitivity for the HL-LHC compared to the results from Run 2.

[

]
with the projected results from the

(lower right),
which results in a sizable exclusion region for
m_{\PX}=900
–1500
\GeV
and
m_{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}}=110
–135
\GeV
.

0.6
Summary

The analyses searching for the production of the Higgs (
\PH
) boson
through decays of heavy resonances, performed by the CMS Collaboration
using the Run 2 data set, are reviewed. This Report covers final states
with two bosons with at least one an
\PH
boson, namely
an
\PH
boson and a vector boson (
\PV\PH
), a pair of
\PH
bosons (
{\PH\PH}
), and an
\PH
boson
joined by a new boson
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
(
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
), where V represents a
\PW
or a
\PZ
boson.

The analyses cover a wide range of
\PH
boson decay modes,
in particular, decays into photons,
\PQb
quarks,
\PGt
leptons, and
\PW
bosons. The
{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}
boson is exclusively searched for in
\PQb
quark final
states. Topologies involving both resolved and merged jet objects are
used to cover a wide range of the phase space. Multivariate methods are
employed in various ways to improve the performance.

The results are presented as summary plots which show the
sensitivity of all channels in direct comparison. For the
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
final states, the results obtained by combining all decay channels
are presented for the first time.

The results are interpreted in the context of various beyond-the-standard model
scenarios for resonances decaying into
\PV\PH
,
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
final states.
These include various extended Higgs sector
models, warped extra-dimension models, and heavy vector triplet
models. The results from resonant
\PH
boson production searches are
compared with results from searches in other channels.

While all presented analyses assume the validity of the
narrow-width approximation, a dedicated study of the impact of finite
width and interference is performed for the first time in CMS
for the real singlet extension of the standard model. This study shows the modification of
the
{\PH\PH}
cross section and line shape in regions of the parameter space
where the narrow-width approximation is not valid anymore.

The expected sensitivity of the analyses in the
{\PH\PH}
and
{{\HepParticle{Y}{}{}}\PH}
final states
is estimated for future data sets with integrated luminosities of 300,
1000, and 3000
\fbinv
, the last number corresponding to the baseline
scenario of the High-Luminosity LHC (HL-LHC) over its full lifetime. The expected upper
limits for resonant
{\PH\PH}
production for the HL-LHC scenario
range from about 50
\unit
fb at a resonance mass of 300
\GeV
to nearly
0.01
\unit
fb for masses of 3
\TeV
and above. The exclusions in terms of
\tanb
in the hMSSM and
M^{125}_{\text{h,EFT}}
scenarios are expanded by almost a
factor of two compared to the Run 2 data set.

This review shows how the specific strengths of many different
experimental signatures can be combined to chart very thoroughly the
territory where resonant Higgs boson production might reveal beyond the standard model physics,
and gives a promising outlook towards the achievement
potential of future measurements in this sector.

Glossary

CP
-odd Higgs bosons in extended Higgs sector models

A Toroidal LHC Apparatus

Boosted decision tree

Beyond the standard model

Confidence level

Compact Muon Solenoid

CP

Charge-parity (symmetry)

CR

Control region

Designing decorrelated taggers (procedure)

Deep neural network

Deep tau (identification algorithm)

DY

Drell–Yan (process)

Electromagnetic calorimeter

EFT

Effective field theory

EW

Electroweak

Graviton

ggF

Gluon-gluon fusion (production process)

Hadron calorimeter

Higgs boson pair

High-Luminosity Large Hadron Collider

hMSSM

Habeat MSSM (scenario)

HVT

Heavy vector triplet (model)

Kaluza-Klein (graviton)

Large Hadron Collider

Leading order

Monte Carlo (simulation)

Minimal supersymmetric standard model

Multi-variate analysis

Next-to-leading order

Neural network

Narrow-width approximation

Next-to-minimal 2HDM

Particle flow (method of reconstructing particle candidates)

Proton-proton

Pileup

Pileup-per-particle identification (algorithm)

Quantum chromo-dynamics

\Delta\eta,\Delta\phi
) space

Randall–Sundrum (model)

The second run of the LHC, during the years 2015–2018

Signal region

Supersymmetry

or
